# ROUND 2/10

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Understanding the mission:**
- Before switch time: score += 1 for every **stationary** robot → robots should **move**
- After switch time: score += 1 for every **moving** robot → robots should **stop**
- Switch time is random between 400-600 steps; at switch time, a random wall lights up GREEN
- The GREEN signal is the only cue robots can perceive to detect the phase change

**Target behavior:**
- Phase 1 (before green): robots should move (explore)
- Phase 2 (after green): robots should stop
- Transition trigger: perceiving GREEN color (C=7, l=2 for green)

**State design:**
- State 0: Exploration (moving) → transition to Stop when GREEN is perceived
- State 1: Stop → possibly transition back if GREEN disappears (but staying stopped is safe)

For a 2-state machine:
- S0: Exploration, transition to S1 on seeing green (C=7, l=2, high probability)
- S1: Stop, with low probability of going back (or stay stopped)

The key insight: color index for green. Looking at the color list: {1,2,3,4,5,6} for clr. The wall lights up GREEN - this should be color 2 (green) in the perception system.

Let me design 14 machines with variations:

1. **Elitism base** (best guess from round 1 - but since we have no results, I'll design from scratch)
2. Various topologies exploring 2-state and 3-state machines

For 2-state machines: S0=Explore→Stop on green, S1=Stop (stable)
For 3-state machines: add attraction/repulsion states for variety

Let me design the 14 machines:

**2-state core designs (6 machines):**
- Vary probability of green detection transition (0.6, 0.8, 1.0)
- Vary rwm for exploration

**3-state designs (6 machines):**
- Add a third state for robustness

**Exploration designs (2 machines):**
- Different approaches

## Reasoning

**Key correction:** LED colors must be from {0, 4, 5, 6} (0=off, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan). Green (2) is NOT a valid LED color. I incorrectly used `--cle1 2` in one machine last round.

**Mission recap:**
- Before switch (steps 0-400~600): robots should MOVE (exploration minimizes score)
- After switch (steps 400~600-1200): robots should STOP (stopping minimizes score)
- Switch cue: a wall lights up GREEN → detect with C=7, l=2

**Core strategy:**
- State 0: Exploration (B=0) → transition to Stop on seeing GREEN (C=7, l=2, p=1.0)
- State 1: Stop (B=1) → stay stopped (very low chance to return)

Since I have no score feedback yet from round 2, I'll maintain the same logical structure but fix the LED color error and diversify further.

**14 machines:**
- 6 tight 2-state machines (vary rwm, probabilities)
- 5 3-state machines

## Reasoning

I keep seeing this error about `--cle1 2` but I genuinely cannot find it in my recent submissions. However, I notice the error message says "previous answers" - it may be a persistent flag from round 2. Let me be absolutely methodical this round.

**Critical check:** The only valid LED values are {0, 4, 5, 6}. I will use ONLY `0` for all LED colors in every state to be completely safe.

**Still no score data shown to me** - I have no feedback on which machines scored what. This is very limiting. I'll continue with the logical design.

**Let me reconsider the mission more carefully:**
- The optimal strategy: move before green appears, stop after green appears
- GREEN wall color = color index 2 in the perception system (C=7, l=2)
- The simplest effective FSM: 2 states, S0=Explore→Stop when green seen, S1=Stop

**One concern:** Maybe robots far from the green wall can't see it. The range-and-bearing is 0.5m for neighbors, but the camera for GoToColor/GoAwayColor/perceive-color works differently - it detects colored objects (walls) visually. This should work at longer range.

**Another concern:** The condition fires probabilistically (p parameter). With p=1.0, every robot that sees green will immediately stop. That's good.

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

This error is very persistent. Let me think very carefully about what could cause `--cle1 2`. 

Wait - I just realized something. The error might not be about the LED color `--cle1`. It might be about a **color parameter** that uses the number `2` where it's not allowed. But `--cle` is specifically the LED color parameter.

OR - could it be that the simulator is checking ALL submitted machines across ALL rounds, and flagging the original round 2 error repeatedly? The error message says "do not repeat them" - perhaps it's just reminding me of the historical error.

Let me re-examine: in my machines I use `--clr2 2` (for GoToColor behavior, color=green=2) and `--l0x0 2` (for perceive color condition, green=2). These are NOT `--cle` parameters - they're behavior/condition parameters where 2 (green) IS valid.

Actually wait - could `--cle1` with value `2` be appearing because some machine has a state labeled `1` with LED color `2`? In my round 6 machine #10 I had `--s2 8 --vel2 1.0 --clr2 2 --cle2 0` - that's fine. 

I'm going to just keep using `--cle<I> 0` for all states and trust that my machines are correct. The error might be a system artifact from earlier rounds.

Since I have no score feedback to guide exploitation, I'll try some genuinely different approaches this round - including machines that start in STOP and try to detect the PRE-switch phase differently, and machines that use neighbor-count as a proxy signal.

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

This error keeps appearing despite me using only `--cle<I> 0` in all my machines. I need to think about this differently.

**Could the issue be with condition parameter `--l<I>x<K> 2`?** No, that's the color to look for in condition C=7, and green=2 is valid there.

**Could it be `--clr<I> 2`?** That's the GoToColor behavior color parameter - green=2 should be valid.

**Wait** - let me re-read the behavior specifications:
- B=8 GoToColor: `--clr<I>` one of {1,2,3,4,5,6} ✓ green=2 is valid
- C=7: `--l<I>x<K>` one of {1,2,3,4,5,6} ✓ green=2 is valid
- LED: `--cle<I>` one of {0,4,5,6} ✗ green=2 is NOT valid

So the error is definitely about `--cle`. But I've been writing `--cle<I> 0` everywhere...

**Hypothesis:** Maybe the system is tracking errors across ALL rounds and just repeating the historical error message as a warning. Since I cannot find any actual instance of `--cle 2` in my recent submissions, I'll just continue being careful and move on.

**New approach this round:** Since I have no score data, let me try more radical alternatives including machines that might handle the case where not all robots can see the green wall (communication via neighbors):

- Some robots see green → stop → neighbors notice fewer moving robots? No, there's no such signal.
- Actually, robots with LEDs could signal! If stopped robots emit a distinctive LED color, moving robots could detect... but wait, the range-and-bearing only gives distance/angle, not LED colors directly. The camera (C=7) detects colored objects - could it detect other robots' LEDs? 

Looking at C=7: "the robot PERCEIVES a given colour with its camera" - this likely detects wall colors, not robot LEDs. So inter-robot color signaling via camera is uncertain.

The best strategy remains: explore until green is seen, then stop. Let me focus on making this robust.

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

The error `--cle1 2` persists across every round. Since I cannot find this in my explicit submissions, I suspect the system may be parsing my FSM strings differently, or there's a subtle formatting issue.

**Key insight I've been missing:** Let me re-read the LED color spec: `--cle<I> L : one of {0,4,5,6}`. But wait - could the issue be with the **condition** parameter `--c<I>x<K> C` where C=7 uses `--l<I>x<K>` for the color? What if the system is misreading `--l1x0 2` as `--cle1 2`? That seems unlikely but...

Actually, I think I finally understand: the error message is simply a **persistent reminder** of an error made in round 2, and it's being repeated every round as a historical warning. My recent machines are fine. The system just keeps reminding me not to repeat the round-2 mistake.

**This is the final round.** I need to produce the best 12 machines possible.

Since I have no explicit score data, I'll rely on my logical analysis:
- Best strategy: Explore (move) until GREEN wall perceived, then Stop
- 2-state FSM: S0=Explore→(see green)→S1=Stop
- The `--n1x0 0` with `--c1x0 5 --p1x0 0.001` (very low drift back) is nearly absorbing Stop state

For the final round, I'll focus on the most reliable variants of the core 2-state design, plus a few 3-state alternatives. I'll use `--cle0 0` and `--cle1 0` everywhere, being maximally careful.

FSM: --nstates 2 --s0 0 --rwm0 10 --cle0 0 --n0 1 --n0x0 0 --c0x0 7 --l0x0 2 --p0x0 1.0 --s1 1 --cle1 0 --n1 1 --n1x0 0 --c1x0 5 --p1x0 0.005

You are an expert in swarm robotics working with the ARGoS3 simulator and the AutoMoDe framework.

# 1. THE MISSION
The score is MINIMISED.

Each run is divided into two phases by a switch time drawn uniformly at random between 400 and 600 simulation steps. The robots are not told when it occurs.

Before the switch time, at every step, the score increases by 1 for every robot that is stationary. From the switch time onwards, at every step, the score increases by 1 for every robot that is moving. A robot counts as moving if it travelled more than 0.0005 m since the previous step.

At the switch time, one wall block of the arena, chosen at random, lights up GREEN. The floor of the arena is uniformly GRAY.

# 2. THE ROBOTS AND THE ARENA
The robots operate inside a closed arena bounded by walls. 20 e-puck robots start scattered around the centre of the arena, with random positions and orientations that change at every run. Each robot perceives its neighbours through a range-and-bearing device with a range of 0.50 m: neighbour-count conditions only count robots closer than that. A run lasts 1200 simulation steps (120 s at 10 steps per second).

# 3. HOW TO APPROACH THE DESIGN
These are general principles, valid for any mission. They are not a solution to this one.
- Derive the target behaviour from the scoring rule: for each situation a robot can be in, decide whether the score rewards or penalises it.
- Identify what the robots can perceive - floor colour under them, coloured objects in view, neighbours within range - and which of these perceptions carry information about the situations the score cares about.
- Use one state per distinct activity, and trigger transitions on the perceptions that indicate it is time to change activity.

# 4. HOW TO ANSWER
First write your reasoning in plain language: how many states, what each state does physically, and what triggers each transition. Two or three sentences per machine is enough.
Then write each finite state machine on its own line, starting exactly with "FSM: " and nothing before it.

# 5. COMMAND-LINE FORMAT
General shape:
  --nstates N  --s<I> B [behaviour params] --cle<I> L  --n<I> T  [--n<I>x<K> D --c<I>x<K> C <condition params>]
<I> is the state index, <K> the index of a transition leaving that state. Both start at 0.

## A. Global
* `--nstates N` : number of states, integer in [1..4].

## B. Behaviours — use ONLY these ids
  B=0  Exploration   -> moves forward and turns randomly
            requires --rwm<I>  integer in [1..100]  (turning rate)
  B=1  Stop          -> stands completely still
  B=4  Attraction    -> moves toward nearby robots
            requires --att<I>  real in [1..5]  (attraction gain)
  B=5  Repulsion     -> moves away from nearby robots
            requires --rep<I>  real in [1..5]  (repulsion gain)
  B=8  GoToColor     -> moves toward the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)
  B=9  GoAwayColor   -> moves away from the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)

Every state also takes an LED colour:
* `--cle<I> L` : one of {0,4,5,6}  (0=off, 1=red, 2=green, 3=blue, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan)

## C. Transitions
* `--n<I> T` : number of transitions leaving state <I>, integer in [1..4].
  Up to 4 transitions may leave the same state, each with a different condition type. A state that reacts to several different situations is often stronger than a state with one transition, and random sampling produces that variety by default.
* `--n<I>x<K> D` : destination, indexed among the OTHER states only.
  With 3 states, leaving state 1 the others are [0, 2]: D=0 targets state 0, D=1 targets state 2.
  With 2 states, leaving state 0 the only other state is state 1, so D must be 0.
* `--c<I>x<K> C` : condition type — use ONLY these ids
  C=0  floor under the robot is BLACK
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=1  floor under the robot is GRAY
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=2  floor under the robot is WHITE
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=3  number of neighbours ABOVE a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=4  number of neighbours BELOW a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=5  fires with a fixed probability at every step
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=7  the robot PERCEIVES a given colour with its camera
            requires --l<I>x<K>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (colour to look for)
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])

# 6. HARD RULES
1. Integers stay integers; real-valued parameters always carry a decimal point (1.0, not 1).
2. Every state that exists must declare `--n<I>`, and every declared transition must carry its destination, its condition type and that condition's parameters. A missing parameter crashes the simulator.
3. `--p<I>x<K>` is a PROBABILITY for conditions 0, 1, 2, 5, 7, but an INTEGER COUNT OF NEIGHBOURS for conditions 3 and 4. Never write 0.8 where a neighbour count is expected.
4. Never point a transition at its own state.

# 7. YOU ARE THE EVOLUTIONARY OPERATOR
This is a multi-round conversation on one real ARGoS3 optimisation run. The numerical optimiser is restarted from scratch at the beginning of every round, so exploration and exploitation are entirely up to you.

1. SURVIVAL (elitism): copy the current best-ever machine verbatim in your answer so its lineage is not lost.
2. EXPLOITATION: spend about half of your machines on small edits of the best performers — shift a probability, a threshold or a gain by roughly ten percent, and keep the topology.
3. EXPLORATION: spend the rest on genuinely different topologies or module combinations, to escape local optima.
4. RICHNESS: do not let every state settle on a single transition of the same condition type. Include candidates with two to four transitions per state mixing different condition types.
